<div class='question question'>

A book costs $\pounds 3.40$ and a magazine costs $\pounds 1.60$. Clara spends exactly $\pounds 23$ on books and magazines. 

How many magazines does she buy?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the number of books $= a$

Let the number of magazines $= b$

As per the question,

$
\begin{aligned}
3.4a + 1.6b                          &= 23 \\\\
(3.4a + 1.6b) \times 10              &= 23 \times 10 \\\\
3.4a \times 10 + 1.6b \times 10      &= 23 \times 10 \\\\
34a + 16b                            &= 230
\end{aligned}
$


We can easily infer the values by using trial and error. Let's find out the value of a and b which satisfies the condition above.

| $a$ |  $b$  | $34a + 16b$     |
|:---:|:-----:|----------------:|
|$2$  | $4$   | $132$           | 
|$4$  | $7$   | $238$           |
|$3$  | $8$   | $230$           |

A quick check shows that $a = 3$ and $b = 8$ meets all the criteria above.

Number of magzines Clara buys $= 8$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$8$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the number of books $= a$
<emptyline>
Let the number of magazines $= b$
<emptyline>
As per the question,
<emptyline>
$
\begin{aligned}
3.4a + 1.6b                          &= 23 \\\\
(3.4a + 1.6b) \times 10              &= 23 \times 10 \\\\
3.4a \times 10 + 1.6b \times 10      &= 23 \times 10 \\\\
34a + 16b                            &= 230
\end{aligned}
$
<emptyline>

We can easily infer the values by using trial and error. Let's find out the value of a and b which satisfies the condition above.
<emptyline>
| $a$ |  $b$  | $34a + 16b$     |
|:---:|:-----:|----------------:|
|$2$  | $4$   | $132$           | 
|$4$  | $7$   | $238$           |
|$3$  | $8$   | $230$           |
<emptyline>
A quick check shows that $a = 3$ and $b = 8$ meets all the criteria above.
<emptyline>
Number of magzines Clara buys $= 8$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$8$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

<div class='question question'>

The sum of three different primes is $16$. What are the three numbers?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let three different prime numbers $= a, b, c$

As per the question,

$a + b + c = 16$

We can easily infer the values by using trial and error. Let's find out the value of $a, b$ and $c$ numbers which are less than $16$.

Prime numbers less than $16 = 2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13$

|  $a, b, c$       | $a + b + c$ | Sum $= 16$     |
|:----------------:|:-----------:|:--------------:|
|    $2, 3, 7$     |    $12$     |       No       |
|    $3, 5, 11$    |    $19$     |       No       |
|    $2, 3, 11$    |    $16$     |       Yes      |

A quick check shows that $2$, $3$ and $11$  are the prime numbers which gives a addittion of $= 16$.

Three different prime numbers are $= 2, 3, 11$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$2, 3, 11$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let three different prime numbers $= a, b, c$
<emptyline>
As per the question,
<emptyline>
$a + b + c = 16$
<emptyline>
We can easily infer the values by using trial and error. Let's find out the value of $a, b$ and $c$ numbers which are less than $16$.
<emptyline>
Prime numbers less than $16 = 2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13$
<emptyline>
|  $a, b, c$       | $a + b + c$ | Sum $= 16$     |
|:----------------:|:-----------:|:--------------:|
|    $2, 3, 7$     |    $12$     |       No       |
|    $3, 5, 11$    |    $19$     |       No       |
|    $2, 3, 11$    |    $16$     |       Yes      |
<emptyline>
A quick check shows that $2$, $3$ and $11$  are the prime numbers which gives a addittion of $= 16$.
<emptyline>
Three different prime numbers are $= 2, 3, 11$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$2, 3, 11$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>

<div class='topics'>

<li>
talgebra
</li>
</ul>

<div class='question question'>

Archie thinks of a number. Five sixths of the number is $60$. What is three quarters of the number?

</div>
<div class='workings'>
<div class='working'>

Let the number Archie thinks of $= a$

As per the question,

$
\begin {aligned}
\dfrac{5}{6} \times a  &= 60 \\\\
\dfrac{5 \times a}{6}  &= 60 \\\\
\dfrac{5a}{6}          &= 60 \\\\
5a                     &= 60 \times 6 \\\\
a                      &= \dfrac {60 \times 6} {5} \\\\
a                      &= \dfrac {5 \times 12 \times 6} {5} \\\\
a                      &= \dfrac {\cancel {5} \times 12 \times 6} {\cancel {5}} \\\\
a                      &= 12 \times 6 \\\\
a                      &= 72
\end {aligned}
$

Let's find $\dfrac {3} {4}$ of the number.

$
\begin {aligned} 
&= \dfrac {3} {4} \ \text {of} \ 72 \\\\
&= \dfrac{3} {4} \times 72 \\\\
&= \dfrac{3 \times 72} {4} \\\\
&= \dfrac{3 \times 18 \times 4} {4} \\\\
&= \dfrac{3 \times 18 \times \cancel{4}} {\cancel{4}} \\\\
&= 3 \times 18 \\\\
&= 54
\end{aligned}
$

</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answer'>

$54$

</div>
</div>
<div class='workingscodeblock'>
<div class='workingcodeblock'>

<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>Let the number Archie thinks of $= a$
<emptyline>
As per the question,
<emptyline>
$
\begin {aligned}
\dfrac{5}{6} \times a  &= 60 \\\\
\dfrac{5 \times a}{6}  &= 60 \\\\
\dfrac{5a}{6}          &= 60 \\\\
5a                     &= 60 \times 6 \\\\
a                      &= \dfrac {60 \times 6} {5} \\\\
a                      &= \dfrac {5 \times 12 \times 6} {5} \\\\
a                      &= \dfrac {\cancel {5} \times 12 \times 6} {\cancel {5}} \\\\
a                      &= 12 \times 6 \\\\
a                      &= 72
\end {aligned}
$
<emptyline>
Let's find $\dfrac {3} {4}$ of the number.
<emptyline>
$
\begin {aligned} 
&= \dfrac {3} {4} \ \text {of} \ 72 \\\\
&= \dfrac{3} {4} \times 72 \\\\
&= \dfrac{3 \times 72} {4} \\\\
&= \dfrac{3 \times 18 \times 4} {4} \\\\
&= \dfrac{3 \times 18 \times \cancel{4}} {\cancel{4}} \\\\
&= 3 \times 18 \\\\
&= 54
\end{aligned}
$
</code></pre>
</div>
</div>
<div class='answers'>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>%empty%</code></pre>
</div>
<div class='answercodeblock'>
<pre class='language-latex'><code class='language-latex'>$54$
</code></pre>
</div>
